People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thanks, doctor. I’m here because… well, I’m having a hard time shutting my brain off lately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it feels like one concern turns into ten really fast.\n\nLike if a client doesn’t respond to an email, I’ll start thinking maybe we’re losing the contract, and then somehow I’m worrying about falling behind on divorce payments and whether I’ll ever get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to get control of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts spiral out of control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal work stress, and maybe some of it is, but it gets pretty intense.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m, you know, damaged goods. And something small, like a delayed email from a recruiter, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI mean, I wouldn’t say I’m hiding from everything, but I’ve definitely put off networking follow-ups because I start overthinking how they’ll respond or whether I’ll say the wrong thing. And with client work, sometimes I delay deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I get stuck anticipating all the ways it could go badly.\n\nI’ve also kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, honestly. I’ve been tense and irritable, and I don’t really want people noticing that side of me. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time, exactly. I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it feels like one concern turns into ten really fast.\n\nLike if a client doesn’t respond to an email, I’ll start thinking maybe we’re losing the contract, and then somehow I’m worrying about falling behind on divorce payments and whether I’ll ever get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to get control of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts spiral out of control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal work stress, and maybe some of it is, but it gets pretty intense.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m, you know, damaged goods. And something small, like a delayed email from a recruiter, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI mean, I wouldn’t say I’m hiding from everything, but I’ve definitely put off networking follow-ups because I start overthinking how they’ll respond or whether I’ll say the wrong thing. And with client work, sometimes I delay deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I get stuck anticipating all the ways it could go badly.\n\nI’ve also kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, honestly. I’ve been tense and irritable, and I don’t really want people noticing that side of me. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time, exactly. I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.

I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thanks, doctor. I’m here because… well, I’m having a hard time shutting my brain off lately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it feels like one concern turns into ten really fast.\n\nLike if a client doesn’t respond to an email, I’ll start thinking maybe we’re losing the contract, and then somehow I’m worrying about falling behind on divorce payments and whether I’ll ever get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to get control of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts spiral out of control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal work stress, and maybe some of it is, but it gets pretty intense.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m, you know, damaged goods. And something small, like a delayed email from a recruiter, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I try not to dramatize it, because plenty of people have work stress, but it’s been distressing.\n\nI’ll get so caught up anticipating everything that could go wrong that I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables, which then makes me feel worse. And I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s causing me a lot of distress, even if I don’t always want to admit that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time, exactly. I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I try not to dramatize it, because plenty of people have work stress, but it’s been distressing.\n\nI’ll get so caught up anticipating everything that could go wrong that I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables, which then makes me feel worse. And I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s causing me a lot of distress, even if I don’t always want to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless,” exactly. I mean… I do feel guilty sometimes, like I should be handling things better or not letting people down.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that answer the question?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thanks, doctor. I’m here because… well, I’m having a hard time shutting my brain off lately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it feels like one concern turns into ten really fast.\n\nLike if a client doesn’t respond to an email, I’ll start thinking maybe we’re losing the contract, and then somehow I’m worrying about falling behind on divorce payments and whether I’ll ever get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to get control of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts spiral out of control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal work stress, and maybe some of it is, but it gets pretty intense.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m, you know, damaged goods. And something small, like a delayed email from a recruiter, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI mean, I wouldn’t say I’m hiding from everything, but I’ve definitely put off networking follow-ups because I start overthinking how they’ll respond or whether I’ll say the wrong thing. And with client work, sometimes I delay deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I get stuck anticipating all the ways it could go badly.\n\nI’ve also kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, honestly. I’ve been tense and irritable, and I don’t really want people noticing that side of me. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time, exactly. I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I try not to dramatize it, because plenty of people have work stress, but it’s been distressing.\n\nI’ll get so caught up anticipating everything that could go wrong that I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables, which then makes me feel worse. And I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s causing me a lot of distress, even if I don’t always want to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless,” exactly. I mean… I do feel guilty sometimes, like I should be handling things better or not letting people down.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that answer the question?\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific stressor or event that you feel triggered the start of your worrying and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of one thing, but it really seemed to start after I was fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nI thought it would settle down once I found contract work, and in some ways things did get better, but the worrying never really let up. It’s kind of just stayed with me since then. Is that the kind of trigger you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I try not to dramatize it, because plenty of people have work stress, but it’s been distressing.\n\nI’ll get so caught up anticipating everything that could go wrong that I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables, which then makes me feel worse. And I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s causing me a lot of distress, even if I don’t always want to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless,” exactly. I mean… I do feel guilty sometimes, like I should be handling things better or not letting people down.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that answer the question?\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific stressor or event that you feel triggered the start of your worrying and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of one thing, but it really seemed to start after I was fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nI thought it would settle down once I found contract work, and in some ways things did get better, but the worrying never really let up. It’s kind of just stayed with me since then. Is that the kind of trigger you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform at work or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. So… yes, I guess it is affecting both.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a steady, most-of-the-time way for two weeks. I’ve had low days, sure, but I wouldn’t want to call it depression if that’s not accurate.\n\nMostly it’s more like I get worn down or discouraged, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it feels like one concern turns into ten really fast.\n\nLike if a client doesn’t respond to an email, I’ll start thinking maybe we’re losing the contract, and then somehow I’m worrying about falling behind on divorce payments and whether I’ll ever get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to get control of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts spiral out of control?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal work stress, and maybe some of it is, but it gets pretty intense.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m, you know, damaged goods. And something small, like a delayed email from a recruiter, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI mean, I wouldn’t say I’m hiding from everything, but I’ve definitely put off networking follow-ups because I start overthinking how they’ll respond or whether I’ll say the wrong thing. And with client work, sometimes I delay deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I get stuck anticipating all the ways it could go badly.\n\nI’ve also kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, honestly. I’ve been tense and irritable, and I don’t really want people noticing that side of me. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time, exactly. I mean… I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of low, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down by the worrying, if that makes sense. But “sad or down most of the time” — I don’t know that I’d put it that way. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I still like the same things, at least in theory.\n\nI’ve been less consistent about actually doing them, but I’m not sure that’s because I’ve lost interest. It feels more like I get caught up in my head and then it’s easier to just not go. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts in response to these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way I’d imagine you mean, like rituals or having to do something a certain number of times.\n\nI do replay things in my head sometimes, but I’m not sure if that counts as a “mental act” or if that’s just worrying. I don’t want to mislabel it. Could you maybe say what kind of things you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or actions in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat a thought or action to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI might go over something in my head more than I should, but it doesn’t feel like an urge or a ritual. I hope that answers it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It’s been… probably since I was fired, so about thirteen months ago now. I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some work again, and I did find contract work, but the worrying didn’t really let up the way I hoped it would.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but yeah, it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you fear something specific will happen as a result?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things because I think something specific will happen. It’s more… I might hesitate or put something off when I feel worried, but I don’t have a clear fear like, “If I do this, then that will happen.”\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed a pattern like that. If there’s a particular kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it more carefully.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive thoughts as a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that exact way. When I keep thinking things over, it feels more like I’m trying to understand it or reassure myself, not like, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen.”\n\nI guess there’s a little bit of trying to feel prepared, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as preventing something specific. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying tends to focus on a specific set of concerns or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts around, I think. I mean, there are a few main themes, but my mind kind of jumps between them.\n\nLike one minute it’s the contract work, then it’s mortgage payments, then it’s whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods after what happened. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s the way it can feel in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about the same general topics or does it vary widely?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the same broad areas, I think, but it moves around within them. Work is a big one — the contract stuff, whether things are going to stay steady — and then money, like the mortgage.\n\nAnd then there’s this… probably overthinking social or professional reputation thing, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. I don’t love admitting that, because it sounds a little self-important or paranoid, maybe, but it does come up.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not responding right away, can make my mind jump to, “Okay, this means my career is closing down.” I know that’s a lot to put on one email, but in the moment it feels hard not to go there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worries are usually about everyday, minor things or are they about more significant life events?\n\nPatient: I’d say they’re more about significant life things… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m dealing with disasters every day.\n\nIt’s usually things like contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that’s probably an overreaction, but in the moment it doesn’t feel minor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a major way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has started to interfere.\n\nThere have been times I’ve missed networking follow-ups or put off client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go wrong. I tell myself I’m just being careful, but then the day gets away from me.\n\nAnd socially… I’ve pulled back some. Soccer, seeing friends, that kind of thing. I’m embarrassed, honestly, by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t really want people to see me like that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I try not to dramatize it, because plenty of people have work stress, but it’s been distressing.\n\nI’ll get so caught up anticipating everything that could go wrong that I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables, which then makes me feel worse. And I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s causing me a lot of distress, even if I don’t always want to admit that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless,” exactly. I mean… I do feel guilty sometimes, like I should be handling things better or not letting people down.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean. I’m probably more hard on myself than I should be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that answer the question?\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific stressor or event that you feel triggered the start of your worrying and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of one thing, but it really seemed to start after I was fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nI thought it would settle down once I found contract work, and in some ways things did get better, but the worrying never really let up. It’s kind of just stayed with me since then. Is that the kind of trigger you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worrying is significantly impacting your ability to perform at work or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I mean, I’m still trying to keep things together, but I’ve missed some networking follow-ups and delayed client deliverables because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong.\n\nAnd with relationships, I’ve kind of pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. It’s embarrassing, honestly—I feel tense and irritable, and I don’t want people to see me like that. So… yes, I guess it is affecting both.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a steady, most-of-the-time way for two weeks. I’ve had low days, sure, but I wouldn’t want to call it depression if that’s not accurate.\n\nMostly it’s more like I get worn down or discouraged, if that makes sense. But a full couple of weeks of feeling sad most of the time… I don’t think I can honestly say that. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these excessive and uncontrollable worries for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it’s been more than six months. It started after I was fired, about thirteen months ago, and even once I found some contract work, it didn’t really go away.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down on its own, so maybe I didn’t take it as seriously at first, but… yeah, it’s been going on that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
